People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F98.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because I keep making small mistakes in things I turn in. Like applications or research notes. I’ll think I checked them, but then there’s something embarrassing, like a wrong artwork date or a question I skipped. It’s been happening enough that I figured I should ask someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're paying attention when you're working on these applications or research notes?\n\nPatient: I mean… I feel like I am. That’s the frustrating part. I’ll go back over the application or the notes and think I checked it, but then later there’s still some dumb mistake—like the wrong date on an artwork or a question I just somehow skipped.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily distracted while working on these applications or research notes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. If I’m at my desk trying to read or write, little things can pull me off—like a siren outside, or people talking in the hall. Sometimes it’s just a random thought about an exhibit idea, and then I realize I’ve lost like twenty minutes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I don’t think so. I can sit still most of the time. I haven’t really noticed being restless like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or worried about things lately?\n\nPatient: Um… not especially, I don’t think. I mean, I worry about stuff sometimes, but nothing that feels… constant or out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually double-check your work before submitting it?\n\nPatient: I try to, yeah. That’s kind of the problem… I’ll think I checked it, and then later I notice some small dumb mistake, like a date on an artwork being wrong or I skipped a question on an application. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or belongings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. My apartment gets pretty scattered, and my laptop too. It’s like papers for rent, gallery notes, PDFs, to-do lists… all mixed together. I usually don’t know what to start with first.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, maybe little things sometimes, but I don’t think I’m usually impulsive like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're being careless when you make these mistakes or do you feel like you're just missing them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s carelessness exactly. It feels more like I just… don’t see them, even when I think I’ve checked. Like with applications or research notes, I’ll miss a question or label an artwork date wrong, and then I only notice after it’s already turned in.\n\nDoctor: Do you generally enjoy the tasks you're working on when you're making these mistakes?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. Like the art research stuff or applications, I’m usually trying to do it properly. But I still end up turning things in with little mistakes… skipped questions, wrong artwork dates. That’s kind of what made me come in, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel motivated to do your best when working on these applications or research notes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I’m trying to do them well. That’s why it’s frustrating… I’ll think I checked everything, and then later there’s some small embarrassing thing, like a skipped question or a wrong artwork date.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mistakes are because you're not paying close enough attention to the details of what you're working on?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably it. I don’t mean to, but I miss details. I’ll turn in an application or research notes thinking I checked it, and then there’s a skipped question or a wrong artwork date or something. It’s small, but embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on instructions or completing tasks from start to finish?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I’ll start with good intentions, but finishing everything the way I’m supposed to can slip. I’ve turned things in late, and I missed a couple application deadlines. One of my mentors even said my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for, which was… hard to hear.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you often give up on a task before completing it when it becomes difficult or boring?\n\nPatient: I don’t usually give up exactly… I just put it off. If I know something’s going to take a lot of focus, like a proposal or a dense reading, I’ll suddenly do smaller errands instead. Then it’s the night before and I’m still trying to get it done.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're stopping too early when reviewing your work, before you've caught all the mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’ll look it over and feel like I checked it, but then later there’s still something obvious, like a skipped question or the wrong date on an artwork note. That’s kind of what finally made me come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think if you tried harder, you could catch these mistakes before submitting your work?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe, to a point. But it’s frustrating because I do think I’m trying. I’ll honestly believe I checked an application or my research notes, and then later I notice something small but embarrassing, like a wrong artwork date or a skipped question. So… I don’t know if it’s just “try harder.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’ll go back over the application or the notes and think I checked it, but then later there’s still some dumb mistake—like the wrong date on an artwork or a question I just somehow skipped.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily distracted while working on these applications or research notes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. If I’m at my desk trying to read or write, little things can pull me off—like a siren outside, or people talking in the hall. Sometimes it’s just a random thought about an exhibit idea, and then I realize I’ve lost like twenty minutes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I don’t think so. I can sit still most of the time. That’s kind of the problem… I’ll think I checked it, and then later I notice some small dumb mistake, like a date on an artwork being wrong or I skipped a question on an application. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or belongings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. My apartment gets pretty scattered, and my laptop too. It’s like papers for rent, gallery notes, PDFs, to-do lists… all mixed together. I’ll go back over the application or the notes and think I checked it, but then later there’s still some dumb mistake—like the wrong date on an artwork or a question I just somehow skipped.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily distracted while working on these applications or research notes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. If I’m at my desk trying to read or write, little things can pull me off—like a siren outside, or people talking in the hall. Sometimes it’s just a random thought about an exhibit idea, and then I realize I’ve lost like twenty minutes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I don’t think so. I can sit still most of the time. I haven’t really noticed being restless like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or worried about things lately?\n\nPatient: Um… not especially, I don’t think. I mean, I worry about stuff sometimes, but nothing that feels… constant or out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually double-check your work before submitting it?\n\nPatient: I try to, yeah. That’s kind of the problem… I’ll think I checked it, and then later I notice some small dumb mistake, like a date on an artwork being wrong or I skipped a question on an application. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or belongings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. My apartment gets pretty scattered, and my laptop too. It’s like papers for rent, gallery notes, PDFs, to-do lists… all mixed together. I usually don’t know what to start with first.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, maybe little things sometimes, but I don’t think I’m usually impulsive like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're being careless when you make these mistakes or do you feel like you're just missing them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s carelessness exactly. It feels more like I just… don’t see them, even when I think I’ve checked. Like with applications or research notes, I’ll miss a question or label an artwork date wrong, and then I only notice after it’s already turned in.\n\nDoctor: Do you generally enjoy the tasks you're working on when you're making these mistakes?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. Like the art research stuff or applications, I’m usually trying to do it properly. But I still end up turning things in with little mistakes… skipped questions, wrong artwork dates. That’s kind of what made me come in, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel motivated to do your best when working on these applications or research notes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I’m trying to do them well. That’s why it’s frustrating… I’ll think I checked everything, and then later there’s some small embarrassing thing, like a skipped question or a wrong artwork date.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mistakes are because you're not paying close enough attention to the details of what you're working on?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably it. I don’t mean to, but I miss details. I’ll turn in an application or research notes thinking I checked it, and then there’s a skipped question or a wrong artwork date or something. I’ll start with good intentions, but finishing everything the way I’m supposed to can slip. I’ve turned things in late, and I missed a couple application deadlines. One of my mentors even said my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for, which was… hard to hear.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you often give up on a task before completing it when it becomes difficult or boring?\n\nPatient: I don’t usually give up exactly… I just put it off. If I know something’s going to take a lot of focus, like a proposal or a dense reading, I’ll suddenly do smaller errands instead. Then it’s the night before and I’m still trying to get it done.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're stopping too early when reviewing your work, before you've caught all the mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’ll look it over and feel like I checked it, but then later there’s still something obvious, like a skipped question or the wrong date on an artwork note. That’s kind of what finally made me come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think if you tried harder, you could catch these mistakes before submitting your work?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe, to a point. But it’s frustrating because I do think I’m trying. I’ll honestly believe I checked an application or my research notes, and then later I notice something small but embarrassing, like a wrong artwork date or a skipped question. So… I don’t know if it’s just “try harder.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could avoid making these mistakes if you really put your mind to it?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think so, but… I’m not sure. I do put my mind to it, at least I think I do. I’ll check an application or research notes and feel like it’s fine, then later there’s still something small and embarrassing, like a wrong artwork date or a skipped question. That’s kind of why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're putting in enough effort when working on these applications or research notes?\n\nPatient: I think I am, yeah.
